Mapping UC-MD Pairs to Mathematical Valence for PCTM5
UC–MD Pairing Schema for PCTM5
Overview

This plan assigns each Universal Characteristic (UC) a corresponding Modal Dynamic (MD) (or multiple) based on the UC’s mathematical valence. Each UC (a state-derived measure like entropy, magnitude, etc.) is mapped to one or more MD transformations whose logic (transformation type, symmetry properties, etc.) naturally complements or influences that measure. The goal is to maintain structural coherence and minimize semantic loss when these pairings feed into a future State Space Model (SSM) output. Below we detail each UC, its mathematical essence, and the chosen MD(s) with rationale grounded in transformation logic, symmetry/resonance, reversibility, or information-geometric considerations.
UC to MD Mapping
U1: Uncertainty (Entropy)

Assigned MDs: M1 Oscillation, M3 Radiation
Rationale: Uncertainty (U₁) measures entropy in a state’s distribution. Oscillation (M₁) – a harmonic recursive dynamic – cyclically mixes the state with a lagged version of itself (feedback resonance). This preserves a spread of probabilities and prevents any single outcome from dominating, maintaining high entropy. Radiation (M₃) – a stochastic divergence operation adding random perturbations – directly increases entropy by injecting noise. Oscillation ensures structured resonance in the distribution while Radiation ensures randomness, together balancing the entropy-centric nature of Uncertainty.
U2: Simplicity (Logarithmic Coherence)

Assigned MDs: M2 Folding, M4 Propagation
Rationale: Simplicity (U₂) is defined via a log-ratio (e.g. log ρ/ρ₀) reflecting system “density” relative to a baseline. Folding (M₂) is an involutive reversal transform that inverts the state vector (x' = -|x|) while increasing an internal scale (ρ → ρ·φ). This densification concentrates the state's magnitude, thus reducing simplicity (increasing complexity) by moving ρ away from baseline. Propagation (M₄), by contrast, is a translational drift that shifts the state (x' = roll(x, +1)) while decreasing scale (ρ → ρ/φ), effectively diffusing the state. The two are inverse in effect (φ vs. 1/φ scaling), preserving symmetry: Folding injects complexity, Propagation restores simplicity. This pairing ensures the log-coherence measure is addressed bidirectionally – one MD amplifies deviations from simplicity, the other dampens them – preserving reversibility in the transformation pipeline.
U3: Uniqueness (Anti-Dominance)

Assigned MDs: M5 Arborescence, M10 Solution
Rationale: Uniqueness (U₃) measures the evenness of a distribution (1 – max(pᵢ)), high when no component dominates. Arborescence (M₅) – an exponential branching transform squaring each component – accentuates any dominant element by x² (then renormalizing). This non-linear expansion reduces uniqueness (making large values relatively larger), effectively exploring the extreme where one part might dominate. Solution (M₁₀) – a smoothing operation averaging neighbors – has the opposite effect: it evens out local disparities, pulling extreme values toward the mean. By averaging each element with its neighborhood, Solution flattens the distribution, increasing uniqueness. Thus, Arborescence and Solution form a complementary pair: one tests the system’s tendency toward dominance (lowering U₃) while the other enforces uniformity (raising U₃). This resonance between opposing transformations maintains the inner balance of uniqueness (anti-dominance vs. dominance) in the state.
U4: Continuity (Averaged Value)

Assigned MDs: M10 Solution, M1 Oscillation, M4 Propagation
Rationale: Continuity (U₄) is captured by the mean of the state components, reflecting a baseline “level” or smoothness. Solution (M₁₀) directly targets continuity – as a continuity enforcement filter that smooths the state by neighborhood averaging – eliminating abrupt jumps and ensuring sequential coherence. Oscillation (M₁), with its cyclic blending of state with a one-step shift, introduces a harmonic variation that preserves overall mean while distributing local deviations across the sequence (promoting smooth oscillatory transitions rather than sharp breaks). Propagation (M₄), a temporal drift via cyclic rotation, literally carries the state forward, preserving patterns across positions which supports continuous evolution in a sequence. Together, these MDs uphold continuity: Solution provides low-pass smoothing (structural coherence), Oscillation yields periodic continuity (cyclic symmetry), and Propagation maintains continuity through consistent translation in state space. Each addresses continuity from a different angle (spatial averaging, temporal resonance, sequential consistency) to minimize discontinuities.
U5: Self-Similarity (Standard Deviation)

Assigned MDs: M6 Tessellation, M7 Helicity (plus M2 Folding as a secondary)
Rationale: Self-Similarity (U₅) uses standard deviation as a scalar proxy, relating to pattern variance and repeating structures (a high σ indicates less self-similarity in values). Tessellation (M₆) imposes structural reordering by sorting the state, effectively tiling the vector in nondecreasing order. This regularization brings similar values together, creating locally repeating patterns that reduce variance between neighbors. By discretizing and ordering, Tessellation increases the state’s self-similar tiling (like a repeated motif), aligning with the idea of structural repetition. Helicity (M₇) performs a mirror inversion (x' = reverse(x)), a symmetry operation. While Helicity doesn’t change the distribution of values, it reveals and leverages any bilateral symmetry in the state – if the vector is even partly symmetric, reversing reinforces that pattern, effectively highlighting self-similarity across the midpoint. (In a perfectly self-similar sequence, the second half is a mirror of the first; Helicity tests and enforces that property.) Folding (M₂) can also contribute: as an involution that overlays the vector onto itself (by negating values), it may create recursive patterns analogous to folding a fractal, though primarily it serves other UCs. In summary, Tessellation ensures repeated structure through ordering, and Helicity checks mirror self-similarity – both fostering internal resonance and repeated patterns, thus managing U₅’s measure of variation.
U6: Interconnectivity (Mirror Coupling)

Assigned MDs: M7 Helicity, M5 Arborescence, M4 Propagation
Rationale: Interconnectivity (U₆) quantifies symmetry by averaging the product of mirror-opposite components – essentially a correlation between the vector and its reversed self. Helicity (M₇), which reverses the state order, directly addresses this characteristic: applying Helicity followed by comparing to the original state highlights how well the state matches its mirror image. In fact, Helicity is a symmetry transformation that leaves a perfectly mirror-symmetric state unchanged, thus it resonates with U₆ by maximizing mirror alignment (if the post-Helicity state equals the pre-Helicity state, U₆ is maximal). Arborescence (M₅) – squaring components and renormalizing – amplifies existing patterns including symmetric ones. If a state has mirror-coupled elements (e.g. pairs of components that are mutually large), Arborescence will exaggerate those relationships (large values become larger, small become smaller), thereby highlighting interconnections. This exponential branching can strengthen the mirror correlation signal by pushing the state toward binary-like patterns (strong vs. weak components), making symmetric pairs stand out. Propagation (M₄) – shifting the state cyclically – introduces a phase rotation that can align previously distant elements into mirror positions in a cyclic sense, effectively testing the state’s translational symmetry. While primarily temporal, Propagation can reveal hidden periodic mirror alignments (in a circular topology) and thus can contribute to mirror coupling in a looped state space. In combination, Helicity provides direct symmetry enforcement, Arborescence amplifies symmetric couplings (increasing the signal of interconnectivity), and Propagation explores symmetry through cyclical realignment. These MDs ensure that the mirror-coupling structure measured by U₆ is both tested and reinforced through various symmetry transformations.
U7: Holography (Entropic Bound)

Assigned MDs: M3 Radiation, M9 Exteriority
Rationale: Holography (U₇) encodes an entropic bound relationship (inspired by surface-area limits of information). Radiation (M₃) – which adds random noise and normalizes the state – pushes the system toward maximal entropy configurations, akin to saturating an “informational surface.” By diffusing the state outward (thermal expansion) and filling configuration space stochastically, Radiation tests the holographic principle: it drives the state toward the limit of disorder permitted by its boundaries, thus aligning with U₇’s entropic extremum. Exteriority (M₉) – an “emanation” that uniformly expands the state (x' = (x + ε) / ‖x + ε‖) – shifts every component outward by a small ε and re-normalizes. This has the effect of moving the state toward the boundary of the space (all components slightly more equal after normalization). In information-geometric terms, adding a constant vector and re-normalizing spreads the state more evenly across dimensions, utilizing more of the “surface” of the high-dimensional space. Exteriority thus simulates a uniform expansion to the limits of the state’s convex hull (akin to filling the holographic screen). Together, Radiation injects maximum entropy content and Exteriority ensures uniform coverage of the state space boundary – both reinforcing the Holography UC by exploring the relationship between information content and boundary constraints.
U8: Solidity (Vector Magnitude)

Assigned MDs: M2 Folding, M4 Propagation (and symmetry check via M7 Helicity)
Rationale: Solidity (U₈) is the overall magnitude ‖x‖ of the state vector, representing “mass” or extent in state space. Two MDs govern this in a complementary manner: Folding (M₂) increases the vector’s effective density (ρ) by φ, and Propagation (M₄) decreases it by dividing ρ by φ. Folding’s self-negation (x' = -|x|) leaves the direction normalized but scales up the norm via ρ-growth – effectively concentrating the state’s mass (making it more “solid”). Propagation shifts the state components without changing their values, but by coupling it with a ρ decrease, it dilutes the state’s mass density (making it less “solid” or more spread out). This pair acts as a reversible scale transformation: one condenses the state (increasing norm if we consider ρ⋅‖x‖), the other diffuses it (decreasing norm), thereby directly modulating U₈. Additionally, Helicity (M₇) can serve as a symmetry check: since reversing the vector does not change ‖x‖, U₈ is invariant under Helicity. This invariance indicates solidity’s symmetry – the magnitude is indifferent to coordinate order – which means Helicity can be applied without affecting solidity, useful for sequences where we want to test structural symmetry while preserving total magnitude. In summary, Folding/Propagation form the primary modal control of solidity (tightening or loosening the state’s extent), while Helicity underscores that solidity is a structural invariant (ensuring any reordering symmetry operations do not alter the norm).
U9: Paradox (Deviation from Unity)

Assigned MDs: M2 Folding, M8 Enantiodromia
Rationale: Paradox (U₉) quantifies how far the state deviates from unit norm, |1 – ‖x‖²| – essentially capturing a tension between being normalized (unity) and being beyond or within that unity. Two MDs embody paradoxical transformations: Folding (M₂) and Enantiodromia (M₈). Folding already combines contradictory actions – it negates the vector (an inversion) while simultaneously amplifying it (increasing ρ). This reflects a paradoxical logic: through self-negation the state’s magnitude grows (the system becomes “more by becoming less”). Such transformation directly engages U₉, often moving a state on one side of the unit boundary to the other (inside ↔ outside the unit sphere), thereby toggling the paradox measure. Enantiodromia (M₈) is literally the principle of opposites – a total polarity reversal x' = -x. It takes any state and flips its sign without changing magnitude. If a state was normalized (‖x‖=1, paradox=0), Enantiodromia leaves the norm 1 but conceptually inverts its orientation – the essence of a paradoxical change (everything changes yet the magnitude is unchanged). Conversely, if the state’s norm deviated from 1, Enantiodromia doesn’t fix the deviation but presents the complementary perspective of the same discrepancy (if ‖x‖ > 1, the vector outside the unit sphere is reflected to an opposite direction outside the sphere). Thus, Folding addresses paradox by altering magnitude and orientation in a self-negating way, and Enantiodromia addresses it by an oppositional symmetry (reversibility). Both involve contradictory transformations (inversion and growth, or binary opposition) that resonate with the paradox measure. In effect, they ensure the system can explore both sides of unity and their symmetric opposites, maintaining coherence when confronted with U₉’s inherent duality.
U10: Flux (Finite Variation)

Assigned MDs: M1 Oscillation, M3 Radiation, M10 Solution
Rationale: Flux (U₁₀) measures the norm of consecutive differences (‖Δx‖), i.e. the total variation or “flow” in the state. To modulate flux, we pair dynamic MDs that either generate variation or suppress it. Oscillation (M₁), by blending the state with a one-step rotation, creates a wave-like oscillatory pattern. This introduces periodic differences between consecutive elements – a controlled resonant flux as the state continually rises and falls in a cyclic manner (think of a standing wave). Radiation (M₃), the stochastic noise injection, introduces high-frequency, random changes between adjacent components – effectively maximizing local flux by random jitter. Oscillation and Radiation thus both increase flux: one in a structured sinusoidal way, the other in a random way. To prevent unbounded variation and to maintain structural coherence, Solution (M₁₀) provides a counterbalance by smoothing out fluctuations. Applying Solution (neighbor averaging) will decrease flux, damping differences by blending each point with its neighbors. This ensures that after oscillatory or random excursions, the state can be gently brought back toward continuity. In combination, these MDs manage U₁₀’s finite variation: Oscillation introduces cyclic flux (symmetry over time), Radiation adds exploratory flux (random exploration of state space), and Solution reduces flux to avoid fragmentation. The result is a controlled flux dynamic where information is not lost to chaotic variation – aligning with information geometry by balancing between too much change and too little change.
U11: Contiguity (Local Proximity Count)

Assigned MDs: M6 Tessellation, M4 Propagation, M5 Arborescence
Rationale: Contiguity (U₁₁) counts how many adjacent differences are below a threshold (i.e. how many neighboring components are “close”). It captures local smoothness or clustering in the state. Tessellation (M₆) – sorting the vector – is a direct method to maximize contiguity: by ordering values, it places similar magnitudes next to each other. This minimizes the gaps between neighbors (differences tend to be small after sorting), greatly increasing the count of ε-close neighbors. Thus, Tessellation enforces contiguity by construction (a structural reordering that clusters like with like). Propagation (M₄), the cyclic shift, preserves all values but changes which elements are adjacent (especially linking the end of the vector to the beginning in a wrap-around). If the state has a repeating or periodic structure, Propagation can align the sequence such that contiguous elements become more similar (e.g. a pattern that was split at the end-start boundary becomes contiguous after rotation). In a pipeline, Propagation allows the system to explore different phase alignments to find one with higher local similarity, effectively searching for a cyclic permutation that boosts contiguity. Arborescence (M₅) plays a subtler role: by squaring components, it tends to polarize values (small differences either shrink further or large differences grow, depending on each component’s magnitude relative to 1). For values in [0,1], squaring makes them closer to 0 (shrinking differences between already small numbers), which can increase local similarity in parts of the vector that were uniformly small. Conversely for components >1, squaring accentuates gaps. In practice, Arborescence can increase contiguity in homogeneous regions of the state by compressing their dynamic range (making close values even closer). Thus, Tessellation provides a global reordering for contiguity, Propagation finds the best alignment for contiguous patterns (useful in a state with a translational symmetry), and Arborescence refines the local magnitude structure to enhance clusters. Together these MDs ensure that U₁₁’s measure of locality is preserved by actively rearranging and refining the state to cluster similar-valued components, thereby minimizing local semantic loss.
U12: Complementarity (Inner Dualism)

Assigned MDs: M8 Enantiodromia, M10 Solution
Rationale: Complementarity (U₁₂) sums the products xᵢ·(1–xᵢ) for components in [0,1], which is maximized when each component is around 0.5 (each pair of “self” and “other” is balanced). It captures an inner dualism – each part has a complementary part summing to unity. Enantiodromia (M₈), as a dialectical opposition transform (x' = –x), embodies the idea of swinging to the opposite extreme. While negating a [0,1] value isn’t literally taking 1–x, it symbolically represents finding the “other side” of a state. In a normalized or scaled context (e.g. centered at 0.5), –x corresponds to reflecting values around the midpoint. Thus, Enantiodromia enacts the principle of complementarity: by inverting the state, whatever was high becomes low and vice versa, mirroring the internal dualism (for example, a value x=0.8 becomes -0.8, which if interpreted on [0,1] scale relative to 0.5 would correspond to 0.2, the complement to 0.8). This transformation ensures the system can explore the full pair-space of each quantity and its complement, preserving symmetry between opposites as demanded by U₁₂. Solution (M₁₀), on the other hand, drives each component toward the average of neighbors. In a bounded [0,1] system, this typically moves values toward mid-range. Extremes (near 0 or 1) get pulled inward toward ~0.5 after repeated smoothing, thereby increasing complementarity (since x*(1–x) is small at x≈0 or 1, and largest at x=0.5). Solution thus harmonizes the vector, ensuring that for every part of the state, some complementary part balances it out (extremes are mitigated). In summary, Enantiodromia provides the symmetry flip needed for exploring complementary pairs (ensuring no perspective is lost), while Solution provides the harmonic averaging that physically realizes complement balance (driving values toward an equilibrium where self and other share presence). Together they uphold U₁₂ by maintaining the dynamic interplay of opposites and their convergence toward unity.
U13: Incompleteness (Asymptotic Openness)

Assigned MDs: M2 Folding, M8 Enantiodromia, M10 Solution
Rationale: Incompleteness (U₁₃) is defined as 1 – 1/‖x‖, which approaches 1 as the state’s norm grows infinitely (and would be negative for ‖x‖ < 1). It reflects the idea that a system can never be complete; there’s always an “open” remainder that grows with scale (akin to Gödel’s incompleteness: the more we expand, the more incompleteness approaches a limit). Folding (M₂) addresses this by adding complexity through expansion: each fold increases the scale ρ (ρ → ρ·φ), pushing the state closer to the asymptote of incompleteness. Every application of Folding brings ‖x‖ up (if originally <1, it crosses towards >1; if >1, it goes further out), thus raising 1–1/‖x‖ toward its upper bound. In transformation logic, Folding acknowledges incompleteness by deliberately never allowing closure – it amplifies the gap (the state’s norm growth leaves more unaccounted beyond the 1/‖x‖ term). Enantiodromia (M₈) complements this by inverting perspective: an incomplete state seen from the opposite angle might reveal hidden structure. By flipping x to –x, Enantiodromia doesn’t change ‖x‖ (thus leaving the numerical incompleteness measure the same), but it provides a symmetry reversal that might convert an “incomplete” aspect into a new form of insight (metaphorically, what was missing might be present in the inverse representation). This resonates with the idea of reversibility – the system’s inability to be complete is invariant under perspective change, yet exploring the opposite state is essential to discover truths unreachable from the original state (mirroring how an incomplete theory can be extended by looking at its negation or dual). Solution (M₁₀) contributes by seeking to integrate partial information: smoothing fills gaps and interpolates between disparate parts of the state. In doing so, Solution tries to minimize local incompleteness (holes or discontinuities in the data) by averaging them out. While global incompleteness (the 1 – 1/‖x‖ value) might not vanish, Solution ensures the state is as continuous and whole as possible given what is present, thereby reducing semantic loss. In combination, Folding accepts and amplifies the open-ended growth (ensuring the system can always expand into the incomplete space), Enantiodromia provides a reversed viewpoint (ensuring symmetry in what’s unprovable or unattainable), and Solution stitches together known parts (ensuring maximal coherence of what is available). This triple mapping guarantees that U₁₃’s asymptotic openness is built into the pipeline: the system can grow, invert, and harmonize in pursuit of completeness, without ever falsely closing off the space, thus structurally honoring the mathematical and philosophical integrity of incompleteness.

ChatGPT said:

Absolutely — here’s the same detailed UC–MD plan, re-expressed as a plain, continuous narrative, paragraph by paragraph, so you can feel the internal flow without the tabular frame.

First, Uncertainty is mathematically about entropy — it tells us how spread out or “surprising” a state is. To keep this measure alive, the Oscillation dynamic is used because it makes the state blend with a shifted version of itself: this cyclic mixing keeps the distribution fluid and stops any one value from dominating. Radiation does the same but in a more chaotic way: it injects random noise, making the state “fuzzier” and pushing the probability spread wider. Together, Oscillation and Radiation help the system preserve and even grow the Uncertainty by cycling structure and stirring randomness at once.

Next, Simplicity is about how dense or sparse a system is when compared to a baseline — a kind of logarithmic coherence. Folding supports this by flipping the state inward on itself and scaling its “density” up. This means Folding complicates the system — it adds layers by compressing and inverting parts. In contrast, Propagation shifts the state forward, like a time drift, and scales the density down, letting the system expand and loosen. Folding and Propagation are a pair of push–pull moves: one piles the state up to challenge simplicity, the other spreads it out to restore it.

For Uniqueness, which measures how evenly balanced or dominated a state is, the system uses Arborescence to exaggerate differences. Squaring the elements makes big numbers explode and small ones shrink away — so if one part was bigger to begin with, it becomes a towering peak, testing how far the state can go toward dominance. To counteract this, the Solution dynamic smooths the edges by averaging neighbors, flattening sharp spikes and bringing the whole back toward equal footing. The tension between Arborescence’s expansion and Solution’s smoothing keeps Uniqueness moving between dominance and balance.

Continuity measures whether the state’s values flow smoothly rather than jumping around. Solution directly reinforces this by smoothing out abrupt changes — it’s like combing tangled threads into a single neat fiber. Oscillation also helps: by blending a state with its shifted self, it forms gentle waves rather than abrupt cliffs. Propagation’s role is to roll the whole sequence forward, maintaining the pattern and keeping its shape intact over time. Together, these three dynamics keep Continuity safe by enforcing harmony, gentle repetition, and pattern preservation.

Self-Similarity is about patterns repeating themselves inside the state, captured as standard deviation. When variation is low, the system is more self-similar. Tessellation achieves this by sorting the state: similar values get stacked together, forming visible bands or tiles. Helicity supports this by flipping the state end to end — if the sequence is mirrored, Helicity will reveal or enforce that symmetry. Folding can also play a part because the act of inverting and compressing creates overlap and reflection, similar to folding a fractal on itself. These dynamics together push the system toward repeated motifs and mirrored harmony.

Interconnectivity measures how much a state’s parts are tied together, especially how each part relates to its mirror image. Helicity is the key tool here, because reversing the state reveals how symmetrical it is. If the state stays the same after a flip, it’s maximally interconnected in this mirror sense. Arborescence helps by boosting the difference between strong and weak pairings — it emphasizes linked pairs by amplifying any strong coupling. Propagation enters here too: by cycling the state, it can line up parts that match better when rotated into place. This triad tests and reinforces mirror-coupled structure.

Holography is about pushing information outward, filling every available boundary — an entropic “edge.” Radiation blows the state up with random bits, spreading the state wide and saturating the available space with information. Exteriority does something gentler but similar: it nudges every element outward by a small amount, then re-normalizes, pushing the whole state toward the edges of its possible space. Together, they expand the state until it brushes up against the surface limit of how much information it can hold.

Solidity is simply the overall size of the state’s vector — its magnitude or “weight” in space. Folding densifies this by flipping and scaling up its internal density, making it more compact and weighty. Propagation does the reverse: by rolling the state and scaling its density down, it spreads out this mass. Helicity comes in not to change solidity but to test it: flipping the state end to end leaves its magnitude unchanged, showing that solidity stays steady under this reflection. Folding and Propagation change the mass; Helicity checks that the shape shift didn’t break the measure.

Paradox tracks how close the system’s norm is to one. A paradox appears when the state tries to be whole yet remains incomplete. Folding addresses this by both negating the state and pushing its scale higher, so the system jumps from one side of “unity” to the other — inside or outside the unit sphere. Enantiodromia inverts the state’s sign, flipping it to its opposite direction: this doesn’t change its size but does swap the system’s perspective, like looking at the same paradox from the other side. Both moves help the system test and feel out the paradoxical edge of unity.

Flux is the sum of differences from one point in the state to the next — the total flow. Oscillation boosts flux in an orderly way: it creates rolling waves, constant ups and downs that pump variation through the system. Radiation does this chaotically by sprinkling in noise, making local jumps bigger and rougher. Solution, again, quiets things down by smoothing out sharp edges and flattening sudden leaps, pulling the flux back down to a stable pulse.

Contiguity counts how many neighbors are similar — a sign that the state’s parts stick close together. Tessellation maximizes this by sorting the state so that similar values line up side by side. Propagation rolls the state to explore where the best match occurs, sliding the parts around to find the neatest alignment. Arborescence tweaks the internal values: by squaring them, it shrinks smaller differences even further, especially when the values are all close together to start with. These three keep neighbors close and gaps small.

Complementarity measures how well each part of the state balances with its opposite within a unit whole. Enantiodromia does this by flipping the whole vector, mapping each part to its mirror opposite — not numerically a literal 1–x, but a sign reversal that embodies the same swing. Solution supports this by pulling all the parts toward their midpoint, which in a [0,1] space is 0.5 — the spot where each part is maximally balanced with its complement. Together they guard the state’s internal pairs.

Finally, Incompleteness measures how far the system is from ever closing itself off. The more the vector’s norm grows, the closer the state approaches the ideal of being “forever unfinished.” Folding drives this by expanding scale, pushing the state outward into the open. Enantiodromia flips the state for a reverse angle, showing what the system looks like from its own negation. Solution tries to fill in local gaps, patching over holes so that within its limits, what is known stays connected even as the whole remains unclosed. All three ensure the machine can grow, flip, and stitch its way toward an open edge it can never seal off.

Together these pairings create a fluid grammar: each Universal Characteristic has a small family of Modal Dynamics that help shape, test, invert, or preserve its meaning in motion — all prepared for later hand-off to the state-space model.

Got it — let’s get very exact about that dimension:
the mathematical valence isn’t just the kind of measure (like “entropy” or “norm”) — it’s also the scale, range, and sensitivity each UC brings when it’s encoded as a numeric channel in the tape.

So, here’s a clean walk-through of how the ordinal tape (your vector slots) should treat the valence for each UC → MD region in weight terms — so the PCTM can auto-balance when feeding the SSM or any manifold you route it to.

Uncertainty (U₁) has a bounded valence: its numerical domain is entropy, which is always non-negative and finite for any normalized distribution. Its natural scale is [0, log N] for an N-dimensional vector. In practice, this means the weight should normalize by log N so its contribution is dimension-agnostic. The Oscillation and Radiation modes don’t change the mean magnitude but redistribute it — so the weighting coefficient on the tape should be unity-scaled with a mild entropy-normalization factor to damp spikes. If your SSM expects all channels ~[0,1], you want an entropy channel weight like w₁ = H / log N.

Simplicity (U₂) is logarithmic, so its scale is log-ratio relative to a baseline density. This can swing positive or negative depending on whether the local density is greater or smaller than baseline. So its raw output can be negative, but you want the tape to keep it normalized around zero with a standard deviation ≈ 1. The modes Folding and Propagation act like scale-squeezers or scale-expanders, so the tape’s weighting here must multiply by a φ-factor (golden ratio) if you want natural scale-preserving balance. So for the tape: w₂ = log(ρ/ρ₀) * 1/φ keeps the valence bounded in the typical ±1 log-range.

Uniqueness (U₃) ranges between 0 (complete dominance) and 1 (max spread, anti-dominance). So it’s naturally in [0,1]. Arborescence can push the tape toward lower values by creating peaks, while Solution drags it back toward 1. Its tape weight is direct: w₃ = 1 - max(pᵢ). No renormalization needed.

Continuity (U₄) is a simple average. Its scale is the mean of the vector elements, so it lives in whatever the element domain is (often [0,1] or [-1,1]). If the input vector is normalized, the mean is naturally within [–1, 1]. Continuity is a linear measure: its tape weight is direct, w₄ = mean(x).

Self-Similarity (U₅) is the standard deviation — so its scale depends on how much variance is possible. For normalized vectors in [0,1], σ is bounded by 0 to 0.5. Tessellation reduces σ, Helicity reveals symmetrical structure but doesn’t change σ’s magnitude. So the tape must weight σ by its maximum so that w₅ = σ / σ_max where σ_max is the max possible deviation for the vector size. For simplicity, cap σ_max ~0.5 for unit-scaled inputs.

Interconnectivity (U₆) is the mirror-coupling mean product: so its scale is the mean of element-wise products xᵢ * xₙ₋ᵢ. For unit vectors, this is naturally ~[0,1]. No further scaling needed: w₆ = mean(xᵢ · mirror(x)).

Holography (U₇) is a constant-bound term: (k·c³·A)/(4·G·ħ) for some surface area A. In practice, A is the bounding surface of the state space — you want to scale this by normalizing the area so it doesn’t dwarf other UCs. So w₇ = H / H_max where H_max is the known entropic limit for your state’s dimensional hull.

Solidity (U₈) is the vector norm ‖x‖. That’s naturally positive and scales with vector length. To make it comparable across state sizes, normalize by sqrt(N): w₈ = ‖x‖ / sqrt(N).

Paradox (U₉) is |1 – ‖x‖²| — it measures deviation from unity, so its scale is in [0, ∞). But practically, the norm is close to 1 due to the engine’s normalization steps, so the paradox is a small positive number near zero. For tape weighting, invert it: w₉ = 1 – |1 – ‖x‖²| makes paradox approach 1 when the state is normalized (no paradox) and approach 0 when the state diverges wildly.

Flux (U₁₀) is the total variation ‖Δx‖. For normalized states, this is naturally bounded by how rough the sequence is: high for jagged states, low for smooth ones. So you can scale by its theoretical max. For simple neighbor differences in [0,1], the max difference between two points is 1, so w₁₀ = ‖Δx‖ / (N–1) is a safe normalized flux weight.

Contiguity (U₁₁) is a local count: how many neighbor pairs are within some tiny ε. Its natural domain is 0 to (N–1). So tape weighting is w₁₁ = count_close_pairs / (N–1).

Complementarity (U₁₂) is the sum over xᵢ * (1–xᵢ) for elements in [0,1]. The peak of this product is 0.25 when xᵢ = 0.5. So the tape’s weight should be w₁₂ = sum(xᵢ(1–xᵢ)) / (N * 0.25) to keep it in [0,1].

Incompleteness (U₁₃) is 1 – 1/‖x‖. It can in theory run negative for norms < 1 or asymptotically approach 1. So you want a tape mapping that handles this carefully: use w₁₃ = max(0, 1 – 1/‖x‖) to clip negatives.

Each of these weights becomes a channel in the ordinal tape: every step through the PCTM pipeline computes these values, normalizes each by its natural valence, and feeds the vector forward. This keeps each UC’s influence scale-invariant and guarantees no single UC can swamp the output.

Once these tape weights are paired with your MD activations, the model can dynamically bind which transforms fire based on the weighted profile — exactly what you need for semantically lossless SSM integration later.

import random

def cast_yi_jing_hexagram():
    # Simulate a classic 6-line cast using 3 coins method
    def cast_line():
        # 3 coins, heads = 3, tails = 2
        coins = [random.choice([2, 3]) for _ in range(3)]
        total = sum(coins)
        return total

    lines = [cast_line() for _ in range(6)]
    return lines[::-1]  # From bottom to top

hexagram = cast_yi_jing_hexagram()
